Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:
▪       $26.2 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26
▪       $31.2 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.
This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses.
The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach
Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed the specified funding amount would encompass administrative
costs.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in the election commitment.

                                                                                    2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Jobs and Skills Exchange (JSE) sits within the Department of Premier and Cabinet and provides
Victorian Public Service (VPS) employees with priority access to job opportunities across the VPS. The
government established the JSE in July 2019 to make it easier for staff to find internal opportunities
and for hiring managers to find talent within the VPS.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $8.5 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from funding extending the Job and
Skills Exchange.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The specified funding amount would encompass administrative costs.

2. Costs would be benchmarked against 2022-23 Jobs and Skills Exchange funding levels.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       determined the total funding allocated to the Jobs and Skills Exchange in 2022-23

       applied the total funding in 2022-23 yearly across the duration of the policy.

Data sources

        2022.pdf (dtf.vic.gov.au) . [Accessed 22 December 2022]

       Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
        advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
        Advice 065 - Australian Labor Party - Boosting the Jobs and Skills Exchange - 24 November
        2022.pdf (dtf.vic.gov.au) . [Accessed 22 December 2022]

       Information provided by the Department of Premier and Cabinet

        Publicly-announced policy costing

Bringing back the SEC

Political party                Australian Labor Party - Victorian Branch

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP

Policy commencement            1 January 2023

Policy conclusion              30 June 2027

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP committed to
                               re-establish the State Electricity Commission of Victoria (SEC) – a
                               public non-financial corporation – to build new renewable energy
                               generation projects by:
                               ▪   making an initial equity investment in the SEC of $1 billion
                               ▪   providing $20.0 million from 2022–23 to 2024–25 to establish an
                                   SEC office in Morwell, which includes output expenditure for up to
                                   40 full-time equivalent staff and associated establishment costs.

Election commitment            https://www.dtf.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/document/Election%20
                               Costing%20Request%20045%20-
                               %20Australian%20Labor%20Party%20-

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Bringing back the SEC



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                     2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Victorian Government originally established the State Electricity Commission of Victoria (SEC) in
1918 as a state-owned body responsible for the generation and distribution of the state’s electricity.
The functions and powers of the SEC included the ‘acquisition, disposal of, or trade in electricity’.

In 1993, the government reviewed the structure and operations of the state’s electricity industry. The
review led to the privatisation of the electricity network and, by the mid-to-late 1990s, the government
disaggregated the SEC into separate corporate entities responsible for electricity generation,
transmission and distribution.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $860.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $840.0 million due to the equity investment to establish the SEC

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $20.0 million for establishing the SEC office in Morwell.

▪       $1,020.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $1,000.0 million.

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $20.0 million.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $1,000 million from the commencement of asset works in 2022–23
to completion in 2026–27.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the phasing of the asset investment to establish the SEC.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The equity investment to establish the SEC would begin on 1 July 2023 and would be phased in line
   with the cashflow profile of asset initiatives outlined in the 2022 Labor Financial Statement.

2. The SEC would reinvest any future profits generated into renewable energy projects and would not
   be drawn as dividends.

3. The SEC would be responsible for any ongoing operating costs of the Morwell office beyond
   2024–25.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                                    Bringing back the SEC


Method

When costing the equity investment in the SEC, we based the costs on the capped amount specified in
the election commitment.

When costing the establishment of the SEC office, we:

▪   estimated the average salary, on-costs and operating costs of a VPS employee

▪   applied the average cost for a Victorian Public Service employee to the total number of full-time
    equivalent staff under the policy for each year to 2024–25

▪   applied a part year impact to the first year of this policy component to reflect a proposed 1 January
    2023 commencement date.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable for this policy
component.

Data sources

    request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Request 045 - Australian Labor Party - Putting power back in the hands of Victorians - 17 November
    2022.pdf (dtf.vic.gov.au). [Accessed 13 December 2022]

▪   Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Advice 045 - Putting power back in the hands of Victorians - 23 November 2022.pdf (dtf.vic.gov.au).
    [Accessed 13 December 2022]

▪   Parliament of Australia. 1996. Electricity Industry Restructuring: The State of Play. [OINLINE]
    Available at:

    /rp/rp9798/98rp14 [Accessed on 13 December 2022]

Cheaper energy for Victorians, by Victorians – Work experience

Political party                Australian Labor Party - Victorian Branch

Parliamentary leader           The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP

Policy commencement            1 January 2023

Policy conclusion              Ongoing

Policy summary                 On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP committed to:
                               ▪   support 10,000 school students to undertake one-week work
                                   experience placements each year.
                                   –   The Department of Education would coordinate these
                                       placements
                                   –   Placements would be centrally coordinated through an online
                                       portal to process applications
                               ▪   facilitate visits to schools by public sector professionals.

Election commitment            https://www.dtf.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/document/Election%20
                               Costing%20Request%20033%20-
                               %20Australian%20Labor%20Party%20-
                               %20Cheaper%20energy%20for%20Victorians%2C%20by%20Victorians
                               %20-%20Work%20Experience%20-%2014%20November%202022.pdf

                                       OFFICIAL

                                       Cheaper energy for Victorians, by Victorians – Work experience



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                  2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $5.4 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $4.4 million due to coordinating work experience
            placements for school students and facilitating visits to schools by public sector professionals

        -   an increase in assets of $1.0 million due to creating an online portal to process applications.

▪       $12.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $11.0 million due to coordinating work experience
            placements for school students and facilitating visits to schools by public sector professionals

        -   an increase in assets of $1.0 million due to creating an online portal to process applications.

This cost estimate is sensitive to demand for the work experience placements and the final design of
the online portal.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Students would take up the full offer of 10,000 work experience places each year.

2. Schools would take up the full offer of 1,650 school visits by public sector professionals each year.

3. Government departments would be responsible for nominating work experience vacancies and
   accepting candidates.

Method

When costing this policy we:

▪       estimated the set-up costs of the work experience website based on costs of similar government
        websites

▪       estimated the operational cost of the central work experience placement service based on the
        operational costs of job advertising and matching services

▪       compared the number of secondary students and the number of public sector employees to
        determine that 10,000 work experience places could feasibly be filled each year

▪       continued the work experience placement funding after 2026–27 and indexed it to wage increases
        to reflect the ongoing nature of this part of the commitment.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                               Cheaper energy for Victorians, by Victorians – Work experience


Data sources

    https://www.abs.gov.au/statistics/people/education/schools/latest-release#schools [Accessed
    on 9 January 2023]

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $0.3 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating due to oncosts and remuneration for one VPS
employee to undertake the analysis.

This cost estimate is sensitive to the VPS band the employee is employed at.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The VPS employee would be employed at VPS grade 5.

2. The relevant department would absorb the cost of any administrative or additional resourcing
   requirements.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       estimated the salary band of the new full-time employee, based on VPS modelling

       estimated on-costs and yearly wage escalation based on VPS modelling.

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                               2
                                                  OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

There are 2 types of contingencies in the Victorian budget:

1. Funding not allocated to specific purposes – funding set aside for future government decisions.

2. Decisions made but not yet allocated – funding already earmarked for specific projects, but not yet
   allocated to government departments.

These types of contingencies exist for output and assets, making a total of 4.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $2,200.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $2,600.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to a decrease in operating expenses due to a drawdown on unallocated operating
expenses.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed that the output contingency refers to the budget item ‘Funding
not allocated to specific purposes’ from 2023–24 to 2026–27.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       determined the amounts of contingencies allocated to the output contingency ‘Funding not
        allocated to specific purposes’

▪       incorporated the budget impact of drawing down this funding for other commitments.

Data sources

▪       Department of Treasury and Finance. 2022. 2022 Victorian Pre-Election Budget Update. [ONLINE]
        Available at: https://www.dtf.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/document/2022%20Victorian%20Pre-
        Election%20Budget%20Update.pdf. [Accessed 3 January 2023]

▪       Information provided by the Department of Treasury and Finance

                                                                                                  2
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing




Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $29.9 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects an increase in operating expenses of:

        ­   $4.7 million due to program administration and regional delivery

        ­   $25.2 million due to providing grants for batteries.

       $42.2 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects an increase in operating expenses of:

        ­   $6.2 million due to program administration and regional delivery

        ­   $36.0 million due to providing grants for batteries.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Grants would be available consistent with phasing specified in the election commitment.

2. There would be sufficient demand to fully expend the grant program in aggregate and in each year
   of operation.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in the election commitment.

                                                                 2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Power Saving Bonus is a payment to households to encourage them to compare energy offers.
There have been 3 rounds of these offers, including the current round which opened on 1 July 2022 and
is scheduled to run until 30 June 2023.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $400.0 million across 2022–23
to 2025-26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from offering the $250 power
saving bonus.

The financial implications of this policy are sensitive to the number of households that take up the
offer.




Our approach

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       reviewed and accepted as reasonable the Australian Labor Party’s assumption that 1.6 million
        households would take up the offer, as it is consistent with the number that took up the previous
        Power Saving Bonus offer

       assured the total cost based on the assumed uptake and prescribed funding per household.

        (dtf.vic.gov.au). [Accessed 24 November 2022]

        advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at Microsoft Word -

                                                                     2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $18.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

       $63.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses due to hiring inspectors and monitoring and
enforcement officers.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant department.

2. Employee wages would increase in line with the Victorian Government’s annual cap on wages and
   conditions from 2026–27 onwards.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       estimated employee expenses for 20 inspectors and 5 monitoring and enforcement officers.

       escalated these costs by the relevant indexation.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

        [ONLINE] Available at:
        https://www.fwc.gov.au/documents/documents/agreements/fwa/ae513976.pdf. [Accessed 5
        January 2023]

       Information provided by the Department of Treasury and Finance

       Victorian Government. 2022. Moving to the new Wages Policy 2022. [ONLINE] Available at:
        https://www.vic.gov.au/moving-new-wages-policy-2022. [Accessed 5 January 2023]

                                                                                         2
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Free TAFE is available for Australian citizens, permanent residents, and New Zealand citizens who are:

▪       under 20 years of age (regardless of any other qualifications); or

▪       20 years of age or older and enrolling in a course that is a higher qualification than they’ve
        previously attained; or

▪       unemployed and a client of the Jobs Victoria Employment Network; or

▪       retrenched workers; or

▪       automotive supply chain workers.

Currently, the Free TAFE initiative only provides funding for one tuition-free course per person. Once
they’ve commenced a tuition-free course, students are not eligible for another tuition-free place again,
even if they do not finish the course.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to have no impact on the state’s budgeted net position as it is specified to be
funded from existing departmental appropriations. We made no judgement on the impact to other
functions delivered by the relevant department because of this policy.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election
        commitment

▪       offset these costs by reprioritisation from existing TAFE and training demand contingencies.

Data sources

▪       Australian Labor Party – Victorian Division. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
        request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
        Request 036 - Australian Labor Party - Backing TAFE for the skills Victoria needs - 16 November
        2022.pdf (dtf.vic.gov.au). [Accessed 14 December 2022]

▪       Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
        advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
        Advice 036 - Backing TAFE for the skills Victoria needs - 22 November 2022.pdf (dtf.vic.gov.au).
        [Accessed 14 December 2022]

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $159.5 million across 2022–23
to 2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in assets from projects funded by the Building Better
TAFE Fund.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $159.5 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2025–26.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the final project design and construction profiles of the
TAFE projects.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Costs and phasing of the TAFE projects would be similar to comparable TAFE projects.

2. Delivery of funds from the capped Clean Energy TAFE fund would be equally distributed across the
   duration of the policy.

3. Construction of all TAFE projects would begin in 2023–24.

4. Funding would not cover ongoing operating and maintenance costs.

5. Administration costs would be absorbed by the relevant department.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       identified comparable TAFE projects to those specified in the policy

       estimated the cost and phasing of each TAFE build based off comparable TAFE projects.

Data sources

        request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
        Request 037 - Australian Labor Party - Building Better TAFE Fund - 16 November 2022.pdf
        (dtf.vic.gov.au) . [Accessed 03 January 2023]

       Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
        advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing

        (dtf.vic.gov.au) . [Accessed 03 January 2023]

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $637.5 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

       $850.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in assets for upgrading 96 local schools.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $850.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2026–27.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.

However while the funding amount is certain, the nature and quality of the works may vary due to cost
pressures, such as materials and labour, to maintain the stated cost committed.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The construction timeline and profile would be similar to other comparable projects.

2. All funding would be provided evenly over 4 years towards construction of the school upgrades,
   rather than ongoing operation or maintenance costs.

3. Administrative costs would be absorbed by the Victorian School Building Authority.

Method

When costing this policy, we estimated the cost of school upgrades using similar projects. As a result,
we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

                               providing planning funding for the building of 26 new schools and a

                               Costing%20Request%20040%20-
                               %20Australian%20Labor%20Party%20-
                               %20New%20Schools%20for%20Victorian%20kids%20-
                               %2017%20November%202022.pdf

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  New schools for Victorian kids



Our response

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                             2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $26.0 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses due to the cost of planning funding
for the 26 new schools.

The financial implications of this policy are sensitive to differences in the scope and location-specific
factors of individual schools.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Planning funding would be phased over one year.

2. Funding would be an operating expense.

3. The specified funding amount would encompass administrative costs.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       estimated new school planning funding based on recent examples

       assured that the funding estimate for this policy in the Australian Labor Party’s request for costing
        by the Department of Treasury and Finance, and in the department’s response, is reasonable.

Data sources

       The Hon. Dan Andrews 2022. Great Schools for Armstrong Creek and Mount Duneed. [ONLINE]

Publicly-announced policy costing




Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $12.0 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses due to developing the new VET
certificates and qualifications, and the business case for an SEC Centre of Training Excellence.

We expect workforce development plan to have no impact on the state’s budgeted net position as this
would be met within existing departmental resources.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The cost of developing new VET certificates and other qualifications would be similar to
   comparable initiatives.

2. The capped funding amount for the business case would be exhausted, with funding spent equally
   over 2023–24 to 2024–25.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

1. estimated the cost of the new VET certificates and other qualifications based on similar initiatives

2. based the cost of the business case on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the
   election commitment

3. compared our estimates to and assured the Department of Treasury and Finance’s estimates of the
   cost of this policy.

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Supporting independent kinder



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                            2
                                                           OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Victorian Government funds kindergartens with grants on a per enrolment basis. The standard
funding amount to non-government and independent kindergartens is $3,831 per enrolment.

The Victorian Government provides different funding amounts relative to the standard rate for certain
non-government kindergartens. The funding amount received is based on each kindergarten’s historical
Index of Economic Resources (IER) rating, as determined by the Australian Bureau of Statistics.


                             Grants per enrolment for non-government kindergartens

Non-government kindergarten               Index of Economic           Current funding rate    Share of standard
                                              Resources                ($ per enrolment)       rate funding (%)

Type 1                                                          1-4                     640                 16.7

Type 2                                                         5-12                   2,076                 54.2

Note: Funding rates refer to the Victorian Government funding rate.

To raise state government funding per enrolment for type 1 and type 2 non-government kindergartens
to the standard rate would mean an increase of:

▪       $3,191 per enrolment for type 1 non-government kindergartens

▪       $1,755 per enrolment for type 2 non-government kindergartens.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $39.2 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $173.3 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from the additional funding provided to non-
government kindergartens.

The financial implications of this policy are sensitive to changes in non-government kindergarten
enrolments.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant department.

2. Funding would be an operating expense.

3. The change in funding rates would be applied to Type 1 and Type 2 non-government kindergartens.




    3
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                                                              Supporting independent kinder


4. Kindergarten enrolments would increase in line with growth in the Victorian population.

5. Per capita funding rates would increase in line with the Melbourne consumer price index.

6. Under the policy, the non-government Type 1 kindergarten per-capita funding rate would be
   increased from $640 to the standard rate of $3,831 commencing January 2024.

7. Under the policy, the non-government Type 2 kindergarten per-capita funding rate would be
   increased from $2,076 to the standard rate of $3,831 commencing January 2024.

8. The costing is limited to the per capita funding rate and does not include flow on impacts to or from
   other policies.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined current per capita funding rates and enrolment numbers for Type 1 and Type 2
    kindergartens

▪   estimated kindergarten enrolment growth by applying indexation of the population growth rate

▪   escalated funding rates in line with the Melbourne consumer price index

▪   calculated the impact of the policy by multiplying enrolment numbers by the difference between
    current per-capita funding for Type 1 and Type 2 kindergartens and the standard rate for non-
    government kindergartens

▪   applied a part year impact to the first year of this policy to reflect a proposed 1 January 2024
    commencement date.

Data sources

▪   The Hon. Dan Andrews 2022. Better Facilities for Catholic and Independent Schools. [ONLINE]
    Available at Better Facilities for Catholic and Independent Schools (danandrews.com.au) [Accessed
    15 December 2022]

▪   Australian Labor Party – Victorian Division. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Request 041 - Australian Labor Party - Supporting independent kinder - 17 November 2022.pdf
    (dtf.vic.gov.au) [Accessed 15 December 2022]

▪   Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Advice 041 - Australian Labor Party - Supporting independent kinder (dtf.vic.gov.au). [Accessed 15
    December 2022]

▪   Department of Education and Training Victoria. The Kindergarten Funding Guide [ONLINE]
    Available at The Kindergarten Guide 2016.pdf (education.vic.gov.au) [Accessed 20 December 2022]

                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $87.9 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in assets of $84.3 million to establish the technical schools

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $20.2 million due to the operating costs of the technical
            school, a clean energy equipment fund, and development of the app

        ­   partially offset by a reduction in operating expenses of $16.6 million due to reprioritisations
            from the Tech School Equipment Renewal Fund and internal reprioritisations by the Department
            of Education and Training.

       $98.1 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in assets of $84.3 million to establish the technical schools

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $32.9 million due to the operating costs of the technical
            school, a clean energy equipment fund, and development of the app

        ­   partially offset by a reduction in operating expenses of $19.1 million due to reprioritisations
            from the Tech School Equipment Renewal Fund and internal reprioritisations by the Department
            of Education and Training.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $84.3 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2025–26.

This cost estimate is sensitive to the phasing of the asset expenditure to establish the technical
schools.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The phasing of the asset expenditure to establish the technical schools would be similar to
   comparable initiatives.

2. There would be sufficient available funds for the specified reprioritisations.




    3

                                                                                   Supporting our schools


Method

When costing this policy, we:

   based the cost of the technical schools, clean energy equipment fund, and app development on the
    capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election commitment

   phased the asset expenditure to establish the technical schools based on similar initiatives

   offset costs by the amounts specified in the election commitment.

                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $20 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses due to grants provided to local
councils and truck owners.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions.

1. The administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant department.

2. There would be sufficient demand for grants for replacement trucks each year to fully subscribe the
   program.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election
commitment.

We estimated the number of trucks that would be eligible for grants and have concluded that truck
owners would take up all grants on offer.

Data sources

       Australian Bureau of Statistics. 2021. Motor Vehicle Census, Australia. [ONLINE] Available at:

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Under the Water Industry Act 1994 (Vic), water corporations pay the Environmental Contribution Levy
(ECL) to fund initiatives that promote the sustainable management of water or address adverse water-
related environmental impacts. The Department of Environment, Land, Water and Planning (DELWP)
administers the levy. The fifth tranche of the ECL commenced on 1 July 2020 for a period of 4 years
concluding on 30 June 2024.


                               Environmental Contribution levy revenue forecast

$ million                               2021-22        2022–23      2023–24       2024–25          2025–26
                                        (actual)      (forecast)    (forecast)    (forecast)       (forecast)

Environmental contribution levy              173.5          173.0         173.0                0                0

Source: State Financial Report 2021–22, Victorian Budget 2022–23


Costing overview

We expect this policy to have no net impact on the state’s budgeted net position across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This reflects:

▪       an increase in operating expenses of $10.0 million to establish the grants fund for the revegetation
        and regeneration of waterways

▪       offset by a reduction in operating expenses of $10.0 million due to reprioritising budgeted
        expenditure under ECL tranche 5, including a rephase of $3.1 million from 2022–23 to 2023–24.

This cost estimate is sensitive to the uncommitted amounts of budgeted output expenditure to be
funded by the ECL in 2022–23 and 2023–24.

The financial implications of the grants for the revegetation and regeneration of waterways are certain
as they relate to specified funding. We have not assessed the extent to which the offset would impact
Victorian Government service delivery of reprioritised expenditure from other measures.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The specified funding amount would encompass administrative costs.

2. The offset would be achieved by reprioritising budgeted expenditure of ECL funds.

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                              2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

In the 2022–23 Victorian Budget, the government allocated an average contribution of $994.5 million
per year to 2025–26, towards closed defined benefit schemes. These are retirement plans for former
staff under older pension schemes, and represent unfunded liabilities for the government.

This policy would defer the government’s contribution to 30 June 2027 and would then make up for the
reduced contributions to these schemes.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $2,400.0 million across 2022-23 to 2025-26. This is entirely due to a decrease in asset investment
        from the deferral of the state’s contributions to closed defined benefits schemes.

▪       $314.7 million across 2022-23 to 2032-33. This reflects:

        -   a decrease in asset investment of $3,000.0 million due to the deferral of the state’s
            contributions to closed defined benefits schemes

        -   an increase in asset investment of $2,685.3 million due to an increase in contributions after 30
            June 2027 in order to cover the deferred contributions and foregone investment returns.

This policy is sensitive to actuarial assessments of the differences between the schemes' asset values
and its expected drawdowns over time to ensure that the reduced payments to the schemes are made
up by 2035. We made no judgement on the outcome nor impact of future actuarial assessments for
these schemes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The specified amount would encompass administrative costs.

2. The government would defer contributions towards the state’s unfunded liabilities related to closed
   defined benefits schemes evenly from 2022-23 to 2026-27.

3. The government would repay the real value of deferred amounts through additional contributions
   which would be evenly spread from 1 July 2027 to 30 June 2035.

4. The deferred contribution payments towards the state’s unfunded liabilities would be asset
   investment.




    3
                                               OFFICIAL

                                                             Defined benefit liability management - offsets


Method

When costing this policy we:

▪   based this policy costing on the capped deferred contributions amount specified in the election
    commitment

▪   determined that the specified contributions amount to be deferred each year is reasonable and
    included in the budget for the forward estimates period

▪   escalated the deferred payments by the Melbourne consumer price index

▪   evenly spread out the total amount of deferred contributions from 2022-23 to 2026-27

▪   evenly spread out the repayment of the deferred amount between 2027-28 and 2034-2035.

Data sources

▪   Australian Labor Party – Victorian Division. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Request 077 - Australian Labor Party - Defined benefit liability management - 24 November
    2022.pdf (dtf.vic.gov.au) . [Accessed 24 November 2022]

▪   Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Advice 077 - Australian Labor Party - Defined benefit liability management - 24 November 2022.pdf
    (dtf.vic.gov.au) . [Accessed 24 November 2022]

▪   Information from the Department of Treasury and Finance

▪   Victorian Budget 2022–23, Budget Paper No.5 Statement of Finances 2022-23

                                                  Discretionary funds - offsets



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                            2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Victorian Government departments administer a range of discretionary funds, generally trusts or
accounts, for spending in line with fund objectives. Disbursements may be at the discretion of the
minister or secretary, often under grants programs in line with fund objectives. Some discretionary
funds hold revenue which is hypothecated for specific purposes.

For example, the Department of Treasury and Finance administers the Community Support Fund, which
is funded from gambling revenues, and is required to be spent to address problem gambling, or other
specified areas including drug addiction, financial counselling, youth programs, community
advancement, sport, art and tourism.

Other examples of discretionary funds across the Victorian Government include:

▪       Project Trust, administered by the Department of Energy, Environment and Climate Action

▪       Stronger TAFE Fund, administered by the Department of Education

▪       Regional Health Infrastructure Fund, administered by the Department of Health

▪       Major Events Fund, administered by the Department of Jobs, Skills, Industry and Regions

▪       Better Roads Victoria Trust, administered by the Department of Transport and Planning.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $225.0 million across 2023–24 to 2025–26

▪       $300.0 million across 2023–24 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to a decrease in operating expenses due to reprioritising budgeted expenditure from
discretionary funds.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       changes in discretionary funds balances spent or contractually committed at 1 July 2023

▪       the proportion of budgeted expenditure from discretionary funds which remains uncommitted in
        future years.

We have not assessed the extent to which this policy may impact on other Victorian Government
service delivery.




    3
                                               OFFICIAL

                                                                              Discretionary funds - offsets



Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The offsets would involve reprioritising expenditure from discretionary funds which are budgeted
   but unspent or uncommitted.

2. Reprioritisation of unspent or uncommitted funds would be consistent with relevant fund
   objectives.

3. The proportion of budgeted expenditure from discretionary funds which is uncommitted would
   remain broadly consistent over time.

Method

We based this policy costing on the amount and timing of savings from discretionary grants programs
specified in the costing request.

Data sources

▪   Australian Labor Party – Victorian Division. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Request 076 - Australian Labor Party - Discretionary Funds Offset - 24 November 2022.pdf
    (dtf.vic.gov.au). [Accessed 24 November 2022]

▪   Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Advice 076 - Australian Labor Party - Discretionary Funds Offset - 24 November 2022.pdf
    (dtf.vic.gov.au). [Accessed 24 November 2022]

▪   Information from the Department of Treasury and Finance

Our response

Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                          2
                                                           OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Victorian public financial corporations pay annual dividends to the general government sector. The
Transport Accident Commission pays amounts equivalent to dividends to the general government
sector, which are reported as contributions forming parts of grants revenue.


                      Public financial corporation payments to general government sector

($ million)                                     2022-23               2023-24             2024-25         2025-26

Dividends from PFC sector                                    60                      67              79             70

Transport Accident Commission                              200                   600                900        1,300

Note: This table is from page 42 of the Victorian Pre-Election Budget Update 2022.

Source: Department of Treasury and Finance.


Costing overview

We expect this policy to have no net impact on the state’s budgeted net position across 2022–23 to
2025–26, as it rephases payments budgeted for 2024–25 and 2025–26 into 2022–23 and 2023–24.

We expect the Transport Accident Commission would rephase its budgeted payments in line with the
policy specification, and its forecast insurance funding ratio would remain within the preferred range of
100% to 145% over the forward estimates.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to a specified rephase of payments to
the general government sector.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed that the entire rephasing relates to Transport Accident
Commission payments to the general government sector.

Method

We based this policy costing on the specified amount of rephased funding in the election commitment.

Our response

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                    2
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $10.6 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $7.8 million due to the provision of grants and funding
             for animal welfare programs

        ­   an increase in assets of $2.8 million due to establishing a wildlife hospital

       $13.2 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $10.4 million due to the provision of grants and funding
             for animal welfare programs

        ­   an increase in assets of $2.8 million due to establishing a wildlife hospital

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $2.8 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24 to
completion in the same year.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The timeline for construction of the wildlife hospital would follow similar projects.

2. Apart from the asset expenditure for the wildlife hospital, funding for all other components of the
   animal welfare package would:

            a. be spent evenly over the 4 years from 2023–24

            b. encompass administrative costs.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       based the operating expenses of this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in
        the election commitment

       estimated the construction of the wildlife hospital based on the specified TEI and assumed profile
        based on similar projects.

                                                                                             2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Victorian Government departments:

       hire employees on an ongoing, casual or fixed-term contract basis

       engage consultancy services to provide expert analysis or advice.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $150.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

       $200.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to a decrease in operating expenses due to a reduction in labour hire and
consultants.

This cost estimate is sensitive to the availability of savings on labour hire and consultants in Victorian
Government departments. We have not assessed the extent to which this policy would impact Victorian
Government service delivery.




Our approach

Method

We based this policy costing on the amount of savings on labour hire and consultants and phasing
specified in the election commitment.

                                                                          2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Ethical Clothing Australia (ECA) is an accreditation body that works with local textile, clothing and
footwear (TCF) businesses. ECA audit business’s manufacturing operations from design to dispatch to
ensure that local TCF workers are being paid appropriately, receiving all their legal entitlements and
working in safe conditions.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $3.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

       $4.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from grant funding to ECA.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant
department.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election
commitment.

        request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing

        (dtf.vic.gov.au) . [Accessed 24 November 2022]

       Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
        advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
        Advice 061 - Australian Labor Party - Supporting Ethical Clothing - 24 November 2022.pdf
        (dtf.vic.gov.au) . [Accessed 24 November 2022]

       Ethical Clothing Australia 2022. 2022. About Us | Ethical Clothing Australia. [ONLINE] Available

Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                     2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $26.2 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in revenue of $25.2 million due to providing concessions to veterans

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $1.0 million due to the implementation of the program

▪       $88.8 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   a decrease in revenue of $87.8 million due to providing concessions to veterans

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $1.0 million due to the implementation of the program.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the number of registrations and licences that would be
free for veterans and their use of public transport on Anzac Day and Remembrance Day.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Service Victoria would absorb administrative costs.

2. Discounts under the Victorian Veterans Card would not be applied in addition to other existing
   concessions.

3. Veterans who are over 60 years old receive concession travel and can access the Seniors Card,
   already have free travel on weekends.

4. Veterans eligible for free travel passes are similar to 2015 figures.

5. There would be no budget impact in 2023–24 for free public transport on Anzac Day and
   Remembrance Day, as it is already provided under the 2022–23 budget measure Support for veteran
   transition and wellbeing.

6. Only non-Department of Veteran Affairs (DVA) card holders would be eligible for the car registration
   discount, free fishing and marine licenses and free caravan and trailer registration.

7. Eight per cent of eligible veterans would take up the $100 car registration discount.

8. Five per cent of non-DVA card holders under the age of 70 years currently pay for fishing and
   marine licences.

9. Twenty per cent of eligible veterans would take up either free caravan registration or free trailer
   registration.

10. Eligible veterans would opt for a free standard 5-year marine licences, not a restricted marine
    licence or marine licence with private water craft endorsement.




    3
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                                            Thanking Victorian veterans with cost of living relief


11. The cost of public transport fares, caravan and light trailer registrations, and marine licences
    increase in line with the Melbourne consumer price index.

12. Costs associated with providing access to employment programs would be absorbed by the
    relevant department.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated the veteran population in each year

▪   estimated the cost of public transport fares, annual fishing and marine licenses, and annual
    caravan and trailer registration, and excluded amounts already included in the state budget

▪   calculated the cost of public transport for capped and uncapped fare days

▪   applied the relevant take up of each concession

▪   based the implementation costs on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the
    election commitment.

Data sources

▪   Australian Bureau of Statistics. 2012. 4602.2 – Household Water and Energy Use, Victoria, October
    2011. [ONLINE] Available at: https://www.abs.gov.au/ausstats/abs@.nsf/mf/4602.2. [Accessed 9
    January 2023]

▪   Australian Labor Party – Victorian Division. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Request 028 - Australian Labor Party - Victorian Veterans Card - 11 November 2022.pdf
    (dtf.vic.gov.au). [Accessed 15 December 2022]

▪   Business Victoria. 2023. Victorian public holidays 2023. [ONLINE] Available at:
    https://business.vic.gov.au/business-information/public-holidays/victorian-public-holidays-2023.
    [Accessed 9 January 2023]

    advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Advice 028 - Australian Labor Party - Victorian Veterans Card - 16 November 2022.docx
    (dtf.vic.gov.au). [Accessed 13 December 2022]

    Available at: https://www.premier.vic.gov.au/free-travel-giving-veterans-support-they-deserve.
    [Accessed 9 January 2023]

                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $118.6 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $521.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in assets due to capital works.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $521.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2029–30.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       the final design and timelines for construction of the new hospital

▪       changes in cost escalation rates.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The existing emergency department would remain on the ground floor level. The new tower would
   be built as 6 additional storeys above.

2. Further additional buildings would be constructed, and renovations made to connect, and maintain
   or achieve increased capacities.

3. The hospital operator would co-locate departments within the expanded hospital, including
   relocations.

4. No land acquisition would be required to construct the new hospital.

5. All funding would be for construction of the hospital, rather than for its ongoing operation or
   maintenance costs.

6. Construction timeline and profile would be similar to other comparable hospital projects.

7. The final design of the hospital would include:

        a. the maximum capacity of the specified services where the policy identified an upper bound on
           capacity

        b. a capacity of services consistent with those in similar hospitals for less clearly specified
           services

        c. additional services which are common across Victorian hospitals (such as staff administrative
           spaces, travel and engineering space).




    3
                                               OFFICIAL

                                                                A bigger and better Monash Medical Centre


8. The project costs include a design contingency to account for additional design decisions.

9. There would be no Australian Government funding for this project.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated the size of the hospital in square metres based on policy specifications, comparable
    hospital specifications and dimensions, and existing hospital dimensions

▪   calculated the annual construction cost for the new hospital using comparable project phasing
    profiles.

Data sources

▪   Australian Labor Party – Victorian Division. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
    Request 021 - Australian Labor Party - Hospital Plan for the South-East – Dandenong Hospital - 7
    November 2022 . [Accessed 15 December 2022]

▪   Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
    advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at Election Costing
    Advice 021 - Australian Labor Party - Hospital Plan for the South-East – Dandenong Hospital - 10
    November 2022 . [Accessed 15 December 2022]

    [Accessed 4 January 2023]

                                                      OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $240.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

        -   $194.5 million on the Northern hospital

        -   $45.6 million on the Austin hospital

▪       $1,117.3 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33

        -   $844.9 million on the Northern hospital

        -   $272.6 million on the Austin hospital.

This is entirely due to an increase in assets from the capital works.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $1,117.3 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2029–30.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       the final design and timelines for construction of the new hospitals

▪       changes in cost escalation rates.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. No land acquisition would be required.

2. All funding would be for construction or refurbishment of buildings, rather than for ongoing
   operation or maintenance costs.

3. Construction timeline and profile would be similar to other comparable hospital projects.

4. All development at the Northern Hospital would occupy the current carpark adjacent to
   Cooper Street.

5. The final design of each hospital upgrades would include:

        a. the maximum capacity of the specified services where the policy identified an upper bound on
           capacity

        b. a capacity of services consistent with those in similar hospitals for less clearly specified
           services

        c. refurbishment of additional services which are common areas across Victorian hospitals (such
           as staff accommodation and parking).




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                            A hospital plan for our growing northern suburbs


6. The project costs include a design contingency to account for additional design decisions.

7. There would be no Australian Government funding for this project.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated the size of the hospitals in square metres based on policy specifications, comparable
    hospital specifications and dimensions, and existing hospital dimensions

▪   calculated the annual construction cost for the new hospitals using comparable project phasing
    profiles.

                                            A hospital plan for the south-east – Dandenong Hospital



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $47.2 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $282.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in assets due to capital works.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $282.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2027–28.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       the final design and timelines for construction of the new hospital

▪       changes in cost escalation rates.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. No land acquisition would be required to construct the new hospital.

2. The existing emergency department would be retained.

3. The 2 new floors would be for the outpatient clinic, operating theatres and intensive care units.

4. All funding would be for construction of additional hospital buildings, rather than for its ongoing
   operation or maintenance costs or additional car parks.

5. The construction timeline and profile would be similar to other comparable hospital projects.

6. The project costs include a design contingency to account for additional design decisions.

7. There would be no Australian Government funding for this project.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       estimated the expansion of the emergency department towards the street and towards the carpark

▪       estimated the size of two additional levels above the expanded space

▪       apportioned increased space to intensive care units, operating theatres and outpatient clinics

▪       calculated the annual construction cost for the new hospital using comparable project phasing
        profiles.

                                                  A new ambulance station for Armstrong Creek



Our response

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                          2
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $24.4 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in assets from the capital works and land acquisition.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $24.4 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24 to
completion in 2025–26.

This costing is sensitive to:

       changes in the size, location of the ambulance station, as well as the final design

       changes in land acquisition costs, the size of the parcel of land required, and design variations to
        accommodate specific land parcels.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Funding would be for construction and land acquisition only, and would not include ongoing
   maintenance and administrative costs.

2. The construction profile would be similar to other comparable initiatives to construct an ambulance
   station.

3. The size of the land acquired would be similar to ambulance stations with comparable
   configurations.

4. The land acquisition cost per square metre would be based on prevailing market rates in Greater
   Geelong.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       estimated the construction of the ambulance station based on information provided by the
        Department of Health, adjusted for escalation

       estimated land acquisition costs by applying the average price per square metre for comparable
        land in Greater Geelong to the estimated size of land required to build an ambulance station with
        the specified configurations

       applied additional contingency funding in line with the Department of Treasury and Finance’s
        guidelines to account for site specific construction costs and additional land acquisition costs
        which may arise due to the greenfield development.

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $141.7 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $622.5 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in assets due to capital works.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $622.5 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2029–30.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       the final design and timelines for construction of the new hospital

▪       changes in cost escalation rates.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. All funding would be for construction of the hospital, rather than for its ongoing operation or
   maintenance costs.

2. Construction timeline and profile would be similar to other comparable hospital projects.

3. The final design of the hospital would include:

        a. the maximum capacity of the specified services where the policy identified an upper bound on
           capacity

        b. a capacity of services consistent with those in similar hospitals for less clearly specified
           services

        c. included additional services which are common across Victorian hospitals (e.g. travel,
           engineering space and parking).

4. The project costs include a design contingency to account for additional design decisions.

5. There would be no Australian Government funding for this project.

                                                       A new hospital for West Gippsland to put patients first

▪   estimated the size of the hospital in square metres based on the policy specifications and
    comparable hospital specifications and dimensions

▪   calculated the annual construction cost for the new hospital using comparable project phasing
    profiles.

3. Construction timeline and profile would be similar to other comparable hospital projects.

4. The final design of the hospital would include:

        a. the maximum capacity of the specified services where the policy identified an upper bound on
           capacity

        b. capacity of services consistent with those in similar hospitals for less clearly specified services

        c. refurbishment of additional services which are common across Victorian hospitals (e.g. staff
           accommodation, travel, engineering space and parking).

5. The project costs include a design contingency to account for additional design decisions.

6. There would be no Australian Government funding for this project.




    3
                                               OFFICIAL

                                                       A new Queen Elizabeth II Hospital in Melbourne’s East


Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated the size of the hospital in square metres based on the policy specifications, comparable
    hospital specifications and dimensions, and existing hospital dimensions

▪   calculated the annual construction cost for the new hospital using comparable project phasing
    profiles.

                                                                                             2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $18.9 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses due to pharmacist consultations,
medication reimbursement and program overheads.

Changes to legislation would have no impact on the state’s budgeted net position. The legislative
changes to the Drugs, Poisons and Controlled Substances Act would be met within existing resources.

This cost estimate is sensitive to the:

       number of scripts for medications covered by the policy

       uptake of pharmacy consultations over general practitioner consultations

       reimbursement amount for medicines usually paid by the Australian Government.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The relevant department would absorb administration costs.

2. Approximately 50% of Victorians would opt for a pharmacy consultation rather than a general
   practitioner consultation during the 12-month pilot.

3. No Australian Government PBS contributions would be available.

Method

When costing this policy we:

       estimated the number of scripts for relevant medications using Australian Bureau of Statistics
        population data and related published reports

       estimated the Australian Government contribution for relevant medications under the PBS.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

                                                      OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $54.4 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $189.4 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from providing 3 new Mental Health Wellness
Local centres and planning funds for development of 20 further centres.

This cost estimate is sensitive to the final design, including size and scope, of the Mental Health
Wellness Local centres.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant department.

2. Funding would be provided as operating expense.

3. Services offered will be consistent with the services offered at the existing Mental Health Wellness
   Local centre locations.

4. Services will be delivered within existing Department of Health facilities.

5. Planning additional future Mental Health and Wellbeing Locals centre locations would require the
   relevant department to engage external professional support over 2 years.

        -   The relevant department would absorb any additional costs for planning funds for the future 20
            MWS locations.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       applied an average unit cost for Mental Health Wellness Local centres by region

▪       applied a part year impact to the first of this policy to reflect a proposed 1 April 2024
        commencement date.

                                                                                     2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $17.3 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

       $22.9 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from funding free period products in public
places.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in cost and usage of the sanitary product dispenser
machines and consumables.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Policy would be a 4-year program similar to previous programs.

2. Costs of providing sanitary product dispenser machines would be similar to previous programs.

3. One VPS 5 employee would be required to administer the program.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       estimated the unit cost of providing sanitary products in a dispenser machine based on previous
        programs

       escalated unit cost using Melbourne Consumer Price Index

       calculated the cost of providing 1,500 dispensers in public places

       calculated the cost of one VPS 5 employee required to administer the program across 4 years.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.
