                                           Giving women’s health the focus and funding it deserves



Our response

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                               2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $88.4 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $109.8 million following the introduction of the women’s
            health policy

        ­   an increase in revenue of $21.4 million due to the Australian Government contribution for
            additional laparoscopies.

       $375.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $471.5 million following the introduction of the women’s
            health policy

        ­   an increase in revenue of $96.5 million due to the Australian Government contribution for
            additional laparoscopies.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

       the size and scope of the women’s health clinics and the sexual and reproductive health hubs

       changes in the remuneration and on-costs for staff appointed to the women’s health hubs and the
        sexual and reproductive health hubs

       changes in staff travel allowances and travel patterns for staff appointed to the mobile women’s
        health clinic

       changes in the unit cost of additional laparoscopies, including procedural complexity and individual
        patient characteristics.

The financial implications of the capped component of this policy is certain as it relates to specified
capped funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the
desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant department.

2. Staff remuneration would increase in line with average historical growth.

3. Establishment costs associated with the women’s health clinics and mobile women’s health clinic
   would be based on similar comparable initiatives and escalated by the Melbourne consumer price
   index.

4. Establishment and operating costs associated with the additional sexual and reproductive health
   hubs would be based on existing hubs and escalated by the relevant indexation.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                     Giving women’s health the focus and funding it deserves


5. Travel allowances for staff appointed to the mobile women’s health clinic would be based on the
   current vehicle allowance per kilometre and escalated by the Melbourne consumer price index.

6. Staff appointed to the mobile women’s health clinic would travel 300 km per day on average for 4
   days per week.

7. Costs associated with data collection and research, the inquiry into women’s pain, and the
   Women’s Health Research Institute business case would be based on similar comparable
   initiatives.

8. The Australian government would contribute 45% of the funding for additional laparascopies under
   the policy, in line with the National Health Reform Agreement.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

   determined costs associated with the establishment and operation of similar primary health clinics
    and sexual and reproductive health hubs and escalated these by the relevant indexation

   applied these costs to the establishment and operation of the women’s health clinics and sexual
    and reproductive health hubs

   estimated staffing expenses associated with the women’s health clinics based on the relevant
    enterprise agreements and escalated these by the relevant indexation

   estimated travel allowances for staff appointed to the mobile women’s health clinic based on the
    current vehicle allowance per kilometre and an estimate of average distances travelled per year

   estimated the cost of data collection and research, the inquiry into women’s pain, and the Women’s
    Health Research Institute business case based on similar comparable initiatives

   calculated the cost of additional laparascopies based on the unit cost of the surgery and the
    number of additional surgeries to be provided under the policy

   partially offset the cost of the additional laparascopies by the Australian government’s
    contribution.

For the remaining policy components, we based this costing on the capped amount of funding and
profile specified in the election commitment.

Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                 2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Ambulance Victoria operates vehicles for Mobile Intensive Care Ambulance (MICA) services, including
Mercedes-Benz Sprinter for MICA Ambulance, and other vehicles in MICA support and MICA single
responder roles.

MICA vehicles are staffed by specially-trained MICA paramedics. MICA paramedics have a higher
clinical skill set than other paramedics and can perform more advanced medical procedures.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $25.1 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $23.1 million from the grant and staffing costs for the new
            MICA paramedics

        -   an increase in assets of $2.0 million due to purchasing new vehicles

▪       $134.9 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $132.9 million from the grant and staffing costs for the
            new MICA paramedics

        -   an increase in assets of $2.0 million due to purchasing new vehicles.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $2.0 million from the purchase of vehicles in 2023–24.

This cost estimate for the MICA paramedics is sensitive to the recruitment and retention outcomes of
staff hired under this policy, and to changes in the relevant workplace agreements.

The financial implications of grants in this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding.
We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The specified funding amounts would encompass administrative costs.

2. The government would purchase MICA ambulances, equipment and accessories, including
   ambulance stretchers, at similar expense as other states.

3. Each MICA paramedic would be operationalised for 12-hour shifts over peak periods at additional
   costs of allowances.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                           Massive boost to the next generation of paramedics


4. Hiring each MICA paramedic would require induction costs similar to previous Department of
   Health experience.

5. Funding for the 40 MICA paramedics under this policy would be ongoing and escalate with forecast
   wage price index.

6. Other costs would escalate by the Melbourne consumer price index.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated costs involved in hiring and operationalising MICA paramedics

▪   estimated the cost of MICA ambulances using unit costs to purchase and equip the specified
    number of vehicles

▪   based the grant amounts on funding and phasing specified in the election commitment.

                               Costing%20Request%20018%20-

Our response

Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                      2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $44.0 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This in entirely due to an increase in assets due to the purchase of new PET scanners.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $44.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24.

This cost estimate is sensitive to model of PET scanner purchased and associated unit cost.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The model of scanners to be purchased would be PET-CT scanners.

2. The funding amount would encompass administrative and related auxiliary costs such as full
   installation and delivery of the PET scanner.

3. These scanners would replace existing equipment and would be operated and maintained using
   existing resources and budget allocation.

4. The unit cost of the PET scanner would include construction of radiation proof rooms and screened
   rooms for waiting and treatment.

5. As per the policy specification, no funding would be allocated to hospitals for operation of the PET-
   CT scanners.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       determined the unit cost of a PET scanner based on the amount specified in the election
        commitment

▪       calculated the total cost by applying the unit cost of a PET scanner to the number of scanners to be
        purchased as specified in the election commitment.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable for the policy
specified.

More support for our nurses and midwives

Political party        Australian Labor Party - Victorian Branch

Parliamentary leader   The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP

Policy commencement    1 January 2023

Policy conclusion      30 June 2027

Policy summary         On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP committed:

 3
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                                                    More support for our nurses and midwives


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

   $92.2 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

   $161.6 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from increased staffing and financial support
for nursing.

The cost estimate for the sign-on bonus policy is sensitive to changes in the total number of funded
graduate full-time equivalent staff positions in the health system.

The financial implications of the remaining policies are certain as they relate to specified capped
funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired
outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The specified funding amount would encompass administrative costs.

2. The total number of graduate full-time equivalent staff positions would remain the same.

3. All graduate full-time equivalent staff positions on offer in the public health service would be filled.

4. All participants would complete the 2-year program.

Method

When costing the sign-on bonus policy component, we estimated the total number of graduate full-time
equivalent staff positions based on data gathered from the annual reports of the Department of Health.

For the remaining policy components, we based this costing on the capped amount of funding and
profile specified in the election commitment.

Our response
Budget impact




Costing components

                                                                                             2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

In vitro fertilisation (IVF) is a type of assisted reproductive technology where an egg and sperm are
joined in a laboratory before being transferred by catheter into the uterus. The process is used to assist
with the conception of a child for people who are single or in the gender and sexually diverse
community or to overcome fertility issues.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $29.2 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $54.9 million due to additional funding to increase the
            number of treatment cycles provided and to extend the current program by 2 years

        -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $25.7 million due to Australian Government
            funding.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in:

▪       the cost per IVF treatment cycle

▪       the number of treatment cycles provided.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Extension of current program would include the additional treatment cycles.

2. Additional staff would be required to administer the additional IVF treatment cycles.

3. The number of additional staff would be proportional to the increase in treatment cycles provided.

4. The program would be eligible for Australian Government funding.

5. No additional capital expenditure would be required.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       determined unit cost per IVF cycle using information provided by the Department of Health

▪       determined number of treatment cycles provided each year using information provided by the
        Department of Health

▪       calculated total cost to provide additional treatment cycles and extend program by 2 years.

                               The policy defined low-fee non-government schools as those with less

                               (including fees and other sources of private revenue).
                               The policy would be progressively implemented from 2026 in
                               increments of 20% of eligible schools each year.

                                       OFFICIAL

                                            Smile squad for low-fee Catholic and Independent schools




Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                               2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

The Smile Squad school dental program currently provides free dental care to government school
students. Dental health professionals use specially fitted-out examination and treatment vans to
provide services at participating schools, during school hours.

Students at participating schools receive a free dental pack including a toothbrush, toothpaste, and
oral health brochure. Students can additionally choose to receive a free oral health examination which
may also include:

▪       X-ray services

▪       fluoride treatment

▪       scale and clean treatment

▪       fissure sealant treatment.

Students that require further treatment, such as fillings, can choose to receive this for free during a
subsequent appointment.

The Victorian Government committed funding for the program in the 2019–20 budget. The ‘Free dental
care for government school students’ output initiative included $321.9 million for this purpose. The
program has been progressively implemented across Victoria, with all government schools becoming
eligible from Term 1 2023.

The Australian Government’s Child Dental Benefits Schedule (CDBS) is a benefits program that
supports eligible children with up to $1,052 in basic dental care over a two-year period. The Smile
Squad program endeavours to maximise CDBS rebates to the Victorian Government.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $5.3 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $4.8 million due to ongoing dental service, operations and
            maintenance, and administration costs

        -   an increase in assets of $0.8 million to procure and fit out dental examination and treatment
            vans

        -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $0.3 million in Australian Government rebates

▪       $304.9 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $316.5 million due to ongoing dental service, operations
            and maintenance, and administration costs

        -   an increase in assets of $9.9 million to procure and fit out dental examination and treatment
            vans

        -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $21.4 million in Australian Government rebates.




    3
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                                      Smile squad for low-fee Catholic and Independent schools


The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $9.9 million from the commencement of asset works in 2025–26 to
2032–33.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in:

▪   enrolment demand at non-government schools

▪   the Victorian Government’s definition of low-fee non-government schools and changes in schools’
    levels of private income per student per year

▪   demand for the program from schools and students

▪   the rate of eligible students claiming CDBS rebates for the Victorian Government

▪   the design and fit out of each dental examination and treatment van

▪   cost escalation rates.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The Victorian Government would procure and fit out additional dental examination and treatment
   vans to cater for the increased demand.

2. Dental examination vans would be operated for 5 years and replacement vans would re-use existing
   clinical equipment.

3. The number of Catholic and Independent school students would increase in line with historical
   trend rates.

4. Seventy per cent of eligible non-government schools would participate in the program.

5. Fifty per cent of students at participating Catholic and Independent schools would be examined
   and receive treatment.

6. Additional departmental resources would be required for program management and administration.

7. The program would continue to be eligible for CDBS rebates.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated the number of students at Catholic and Independent schools in Victoria

▪   estimated the number of students at low-fee non-government schools

▪   estimated the number of students eligible for the program each year




                                                                                                         4
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


▪       estimated the number of dental examination and treatment vans required

▪       calculated the costs of procuring, operating and maintaining the dental examination and treatment
        vans

▪       estimated the number of students receiving oral health examinations

▪       estimated the number of students receiving further treatment

▪       calculated the costs of providing dental services to students

▪       estimated the number of students receiving oral health packs

▪       calculated the costs of providing oral health packs to students

▪       calculated the costs of managing and administering the program

▪       calculated the total capital and operational costs of the program

▪       estimated the amount of CDBS rebates that would be claimed

▪       offset costs by revenue from Australian Government CDBS rebates.

We applied a part year impact to the first year of this policy to reflect a proposed 1 January 2026
commencement date.

Context

BlueHub is a service from The Police Association Victoria (TPAV), in association with Phoenix
Australia. It provides expert psychological assessment and treatment of trauma to current Victoria
Police and Victorian-based Australian Federal Police members.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $3.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

       $4.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses from grant funding to BlueHub.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we assumed administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant
department.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election
commitment.

Method

year GP trainee places.

Training and hiring new paramedic practitioners

Political party                Australian Labor Party - Victorian Branch

                               $20.1 million to:

                                  provide 30 scholarships each year over 4 years for paramedic
                                   practitioner students.

Election commitment            https://www.dtf.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/document/Election%20
                               Costing%20Request%20016%20-
                               %20Australian%20Labor%20Party%20-
                               %20Paramedic%20Practitioners%20-%204%20November%202022.pdf

Budget baseline                November 2022 pre-election budget update

Expiry date of this response   At the release date of the next Victorian Government budget or budget
                               update.
                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Training and hiring new paramedic practitioners



Our response

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                              2
                                                      OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $11.5 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $10.4 million due to new paramedic practitioner units and
            scholarships

        ­   an increase in assets of $ 1.1 million due the purchase of new ambulances.

       $20.1 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $19.0 million due to new paramedic practitioner units and
            scholarships

        ­   an increase in assets of $ 1.1 million due the purchase of new ambulances.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $1.1 million in 2025-26.

This cost estimate is sensitive to increases in paramedic wages and the cost of ambulance vehicles.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions based on the policy description:

1. Three paramedic practitioner units would start service in 2025-26, with a fourth starting in 2026-27.

2. Each unit would have a staff allocation of 7.5 FTE.

3. Each unit would have one new vehicle.

4. There would be no additional ambulance station costs.

5. Scholarships would be capped at $30,000, consistent with the policy description.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       estimated the cost of employing additional paramedics based on salaries and on-costs for MICA
        paramedics

       estimated the cost of ambulances based on recent ambulance purchases in South Australia and
        NSW

       estimated administration and set-up costs for the scholarship program and the new paramedic
        practitioner units based on the costs of similar projects in Victoria

       escalated the costs by relevant indexation.




    3
                                              OFFICIAL

                                                           Training and hiring new paramedic practitioners


As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

Data sources

    request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available
    at: https://www.dtf.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/document/Election%20Costing%20Request%200
    16%20-%20Australian%20Labor%20Party%20-%20Paramedic%20Practitioners%20-
    %204%20November%202022.pdf . [Accessed 24 November 2022]

                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $44.6 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $266.4 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in assets from the capital works.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $266.4 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2029–30.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       the final design and timelines for construction of the new hospital

▪       changes in cost escalation rates.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. No additional land acquisition would be required to redevelop and expand the hospital.

2. All funding would be for construction of new infrastructure and refurbishments to the hospital,
   rather than for ongoing operation or maintenance costs.

3. The project would commence 1 July 2023 and the Stage 1 redevelopment would be complete.

4. Ward and clinic sizes would be similar to those with similar purposes in other hospitals.

5. Additional parking would add a new level on existing parking.

6. The construction timeline and profile would be similar to other comparable hospital projects.

7. There would be no Australian Government funding contribution for this project.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       estimated the square metre size of the hospital based on policy specifications, comparable
        hospital specifications and dimensions, and existing hospital dimensions

▪       calculated the annual construction cost for the new hospital using comparable project phasing
        profiles.

                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing




Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $21.4 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $79.1 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses due to continued funding for 50 firefighter
positions.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in firefighter remuneration, and uniform and equipment
costs.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Administration costs associated with transitioning firefighters to full-time positions will be
   absorbed within existing department funding.

2. Firefighter salary would increase in line with state government forecasts and the Melbourne wage
   price index thereafter.

3. Non-wage costs would increase in line with state government forecasts and Melbourne consumer
   price index thereafter.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       determined unit salary and non-wage costs per firefighter

▪       escalated unit costs using the relevant index

▪       calculated total policy cost using escalated unit costs and number of firefighters specified in
        policy.

As a result, we found the estimate of the Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

Delivering emergency services upgrades

                                   land

                               ▪   rebuild Kilmore SES station as a Type A station on the current site

                               ▪   build a new Bannockburn Type B State Emergency Service (SES)
                                   station, including land acquisition

                                   existing Seaford satellite SES station.

                                   Saving Club towards installing a lift and toilet block

                                   Club towards building a new clubhouse and patrol tower

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Delivering emergency services upgrades



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                     2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Country Fire Authority

The CFA is a volunteer-based fire service responsible for responses to fire and other accidents across
regional and rural Victoria. It shares responsibility for Victorian fire services with Fire Rescue Victoria
which employs full-time paid firefighters in major urban areas.

The Type 1B CFA station design has 2 vehicle bays, the Type 2A CFA station design is larger with 3
vehicle bays. Both designs include administrative offices and a local command facility.

Victorian State Emergency Service

Victoria’s SES is a volunteer-based organisation which provides emergency assistance, and
strengthens the community’s capacity to plan for, respond to and recover from emergencies.

The Type A SES station design has 2 vehicle bays, the Type B SES station design is larger with 4 vehicle
bays. Both designs include administrative offices and a training and operations room.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $30.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $22.8 million due to construction of 3 new CFA stations (1 with land
            acquisition), 2 new SES stations, and minor upgrades to 1 SES station

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $7.1 million due to grants to life saving clubs and MSAR,
            and planning for a CFA station upgrade.

▪       $33.7 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $26.7 million due to construction of 3 new CFA stations (1 with land
            acquisition), 2 new SES stations, and minor upgrades to 1 SES station

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $7.1 million due to grants to life saving clubs and MSAR,
            and planning for a CFA station upgrade.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $26.1 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24 to
completion in 2026–27.

The construction costs of CFA and SES stations are sensitive to:

▪       the specific designs of each CFA and SES station, including variations to accommodate specific
        land parcels

▪       land acquisition costs for the Bannockburn SES station and the size of the parcel of land required

▪       the scope of the minor upgrades to the Seaford satellite SES station.

The financial implications of the 4 grant and output components of this policy are certain as they relate
to specified funding.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                    Delivering emergency services upgrades



Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Funding for the CFA and SES stations would be for demolition, land acquisition and construction
   only, and would not include ongoing maintenance and administrative costs.

2. Funding excludes additional costs including temporary relocation costs and town planning.

3. The size of the land acquired for Bannockburn SES station would be similar to other Type B SES
   stations.

4. The land acquisition cost per square metre would be based on prevailing market rates in the Golden
   Plains Shire.

5. The construction profiles would be similar to other comparable initiatives to construct CFA and SES
   stations.

6. The scope of the minor upgrades to Seaford SES station would be developed within the specified
   funding amount.

7. The specified output funding to Queenscliff MSAR and Keysborough CFA would be provided as
   grants.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   estimated the cost of demolishing the Raywood CFA and Kilmore SES stations

▪   estimated land acquisition costs for the Bannockburn SES station based on the average price per
    square metre of land in the Golden Plains Shire and the size of land required to build a Type B SES
    station

▪   estimated the cost of construction of the CFA and SES stations based on information provided by
    the Country Fire Authority and the Department of Justice and Community Services

▪   allocated additional contingency funding to account for site specific construction costs for
    business-as-usual or routine projects

▪   profiled asset expenditure for the CFA and SES stations based on similar projects.

We based the 4 grants and output funding components, and the minor upgrades to Seaford SES
station, on the amounts specified in the election commitment.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable in total, however, it
estimated lower land acquisition costs, appeared to not include demolition costs for the rebuild of
existing stations, and included higher project contingencies than the Department of Treasury and
Finance’s advice on risk, time, cost and contingency for asset investment projects.

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $24.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $69.0 million from providing grants for racing industry

        -   partially offset by a reduction in operating expenses of $45.0 million due to reprioritisations
            from the Victorian Racing Industry Fund

▪       $27.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $87.0 million from providing grants for racing industry

        -   partially offset by a reduction in operating expenses of $60.0 million due to reprioritisations
            from the Victorian Racing Industry Fund.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The specified funding amount would encompass administrative costs.

2. There would be sufficient available funds for the specified reprioritisations.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       based costs on the capped amount of funding and phasing specified in the election commitment

▪       offset costs by the amounts specified in the election commitment.

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $7.4 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This reflects:

       an increase in operating expenses of $5.9 million

       an increase in assets of $1.5 million.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $1.5 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24 to
completion in 2024–25.

The financial implications of this policy are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made
no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Grant recipients would manage the delivery of the upgrades and ongoing costs.

2. Administrative costs would be met within existing resources.

3. Amounts would be spent equally over 2023–24 and 2024–25.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in the election commitment.

 3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                            More piers, jetties and opportunities for little anglers



Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪   $53.7 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

    -   an increase in assets of $54.7 million due to infrastructure investments

    -   partially offset by a reduction in assets of $3.9 million due to sourcing funds from the Better
        Boating Fund

    -   an increase in operating expenses of $22.0 million due to boating and fishing funds

    -   partially offset by a reduction in operating expenses of $19.1 million due to sourcing funds from
        the Better Boating Fund and Recreational Fishing Licence Trust.

▪   $72.8 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

    -   an increase in assets of $72.9 million due to infrastructure investments

    -   partially offset by a reduction in assets of $3.9 million due to sourcing funds from the Better
        Boating Fund

    -   an increase in operating expenses of $29.1 million due to boating and fishing funds.

    -   partially offset by a reduction in operating expenses of $25.3 million due to sourcing funds from
        the Better Boating Fund and Recreational Fishing Licence Trust.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $72.9 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24 to
completion in 2026–27. This policy has an offsetting saving of $3.9 million in reduced asset
investment, meaning it would result in a net increase asset investment by $69.0 million.

The cost estimates for the following components are sensitive to the scope and design of the works:

▪   Tarago Reservoir – Launching facility

▪   Tarago Reservoir – Treatment Plant upgrade

▪   Native Fish Hatchery

▪   Snobs Creek Fish Hatchery

▪   St Leonards Pier, Dromana Pier, Warneet Jetties and Workshops Pier upgrades.

The financial implications of the remaining policies are certain as they relate to specified capped
funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired
outcomes.




                                                                                                              4
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing



Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant department.

2. The cost of boating facilities, pier upgrades and fish hatcheries would be consistent with similar
   comparable projects.

3. Capital expenditure would be spread evenly from 2023–24 to 2026–27.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪       estimated the cost of the asset investment components of this policy based on similar projects and
        escalated these by the relevant indexation

▪       based the costs of the remaining policy components on the capped amount of funding and profile
        specified in the election commitment

▪       offset costs by the amounts specified in the election commitment.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

Data sources

▪       Australian Labor Party – Victorian Division. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
        request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
        Request 044 - Australian Labor Party - More piers, jetties and opportunities for little anglers - 17
        November 2022 . [Accessed 24 November 2022]

▪       Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
        advice | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
        Advice 044 - Australian Labor Party - More piers, jetties and opportunities for little anglers - 22
        November 2022 . [Accessed 24 November 2022]

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by $13.4 million across 2022–23 to
2025–26. This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses due to the provision of grants for
dog parks.

The financial implication for the Dog Park Upgrade Grant program is sensitive to timing and sufficiency
of demand by councils. For all other components of this policy, the financial implications of this policy
are certain as they relate to specified capped funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the
funding specified to achieve the desired outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Funding would be spent evenly over 2023–24 to 2024–25.

2. There would be sufficient demand from local councils for uncapped $4.0 million for the Dog Park
   Upgrade Grant program

3. Funds would be fully expended and encompass administrative costs.

Method

We based this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in the election commitment.

Publicly-announced policy costing



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




 3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                         Supporting our multicultural and multifaith communities



Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪   $63.9 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪   $83.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in operating expenses due to capped grants and school funding.

The cost estimate for the beacon schools offering Punjabi and Hindi language on site as a VCE subject
component of this policy is sensitive to the remuneration of the specialist teachers.

The financial implications of the remaining policies are certain as they relate to specified capped
funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve the desired
outcomes.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Administrative costs would be absorbed by the relevant department.

2. Each beacon school would require 2 specialist teachers to offer Punjabi and Hindi language on site
   as a VCE subject

    -   The teachers would commence work in January 2024.

3. Specialist teachers’ salaries would grow in line with the VPS salary growth rate.

Method

When costing the beacon schools offering Punjabi and Hindi language on site as a VCE subject
component of this policy, we:

▪   estimated the median salary of specialist teachers

▪   escalated the salary cost by relevant indexation

▪   applied a part year impact to the first year of this policy component to reflect a proposed 1 January
    2024 commencement date.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

For the remaining policy components, we based this costing on the capped amount of funding and
profile specified in the election commitment.

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Better aged care services for regional Victorians



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

In Victoria, there are 179 public sector residential aged care services (PSRACS). Public health services
in rural and regional communities operate most services. The Victorian Government contributes
funding for PSRACS, including funding to support the viability of small rural services.

The Victorian Government committed funding for detailed planning and design of the Cohuna public
sector aged care facility redevelopment in the 2021–22 budget. The ‘Rural and Regional Public Sector
Residential Aged Care Services Revitalisation Strategy Stage 1’ initiative included $2.93 million for this
purpose.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $66.6 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $158.4 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in assets from upgrading aged care infrastructure.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $158.4 million from the commencement of asset works in 2024–25
to completion in 2027–28.

This cost estimate is sensitive to:

▪       the final design of each aged care facility upgrade

▪       changes in cost escalation rates.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Construction costs and profiles would be consistent with similar projects.

2. No property or land acquisition would be required.

3. Aged care facility refurbishments would be of a medium-intensity build.

4. In line with similar projects, at least half of the beds delivered would be aged persons mental health
   (APMH) beds.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                              Better aged care services for regional Victorians


Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined which aged care facilities the policy would upgrade

▪   estimated the size and nature of each proposed facility

▪   estimated the costs and profile of delivering the asset works

▪   calculated the amount of funds the policy would cost.

                                                  Cooling our public housing towers



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                2
                                                      OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $105.1 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $100.8 million

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $4.2 million

▪       $162.9 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $136.6 million

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $26.3 million.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $136.6 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2026–27.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the:

▪       type, mix and unit cost of air-conditioners and generators installed

▪       cost to install air-conditioners and generators

▪       wage costs of service and maintenance employees

▪       replacement components and other service and maintenance materials.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The relevant department would absorb the administrative costs.

2. The unit costs of air-conditioners and generators including installation would be consistent with
   industry standards for high-rise public housing apartments.

3. Asset investment would be evenly spread over 2023–24 to 2026–27.

4. Annual maintenance would be conducted across the air-conditioning units consistent with industry
   standards.

5. There would be no change in the costs to service and maintain generators as a result of this policy,
   as these are upgrades rather than installation of new capacity.




    3
                                                 OFFICIAL

                                                                           Cooling our public housing towers


Method

When costing the asset investment component of this policy, we:

▪   identified the number of impacted apartments

▪   escalated unit costs of air-conditioners and generators by the relevant indexation

▪   derived the total cost of generators for each apartment building from the unit cost of installed air-
    conditioners and the identified number of apartments

▪   estimated the volume of service and maintenance jobs per year based on the number of installed
    air-conditioners

▪   estimated the cost of service and maintenance based on industry standards for labour costs
    escalated by the relevant indexation.

Assumptions

                                                                               2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to increase the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $134.8 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in revenue of $161.0 million due to higher tax revenue

        ­   partly offset by an increase in operating expenses of $26.2 million due to hiring employees to
            undertake compliance activities

       $556.4 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in revenue of $655.4 million due to higher tax revenue

        ­   partly offset by an increase in operating expenses of $99.0 million due to hiring employees to
            undertake compliance activities.

This cost estimate is sensitive to the effectiveness of compliance activities undertaken through the
expanded compliance program in increasing tax revenues.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The increase in revenue from expanding the compliance program would increase in line with the
   Melbourne consumer price index from 2027–28 onwards.

2. Employee wages would increase in line with the Victorian Government’s annual cap on wages and
   conditions from 2027–28 onwards.

3. Revenue collected from extending tax compliance is based on the Department of Treasury and
   Finance’s estimate, and tested against similar tax compliance initiatives.

4. The average start date for new employees would be 1 January 2024.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       estimated total employee expenses from hiring 65 Full-Time Equivalent employees

       estimated the increased revenue from expanding tax compliance operations.

We applied a part year impact to the first year of this policy to reflect the staggered hiring of employees
throughout 2023–24.

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $48.0 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

       $60.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in assets due to upgrading the station.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $60.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2022–23 to
completion in 2026–27.

This cost estimate is sensitive to final design of the station.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Cost of the station upgrade would be consistent with similar station upgrades.

2. Cost of the station upgrade would encompass capital costs only.

Method

When costing this policy we:

       estimated the cost of the station using similar projects

       escalated the cost by relevant indexation.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

                                                                                            2
                                                       OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

This policy provides infrastructure for more and longer trains on the Melton line. In doing so it interacts
with the Australian Labor Party – Victorian Branch election policy costing:

▪       More trains for the regions.

        -   It purchases the additional V/Locity carriages that would be used to make future nine-car
            trains, which would operate on an upgraded Melton line.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $357.5 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

▪       $650.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to an increase in assets due to rail infrastructure works.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $650.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2027–28.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in:

▪       the final design and profile of the upgrades

▪       regional rail cost escalation rates.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The upgrades would be similar to previous platform extensions, track realignments and signalling
   upgrades.

2. Increasing the capacity of the stabling yard at Maddingly would not require additional track
   realignment on the Melton line.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                 Bigger, better trains for Melton and the West


Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined the scope of works to lengthen stations platforms based on similar projects

▪   identified complementary works to realigning track, signalling upgrades, and increases to stabling
    yard capacity based on similar projects

▪   estimated the cost of the identified works using outcomes from similar projects and works.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Cheaper public transport fares for the regions



Our response

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                             2
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

This policy would reduce public transport fares for regional services and in doing so it interacts with the
Australian Labor Party – Victorian Branch election policy costing:

       More trains for the regions

The More trains for the regions costing interacts with this policy due to an increased demand for regional
services from discounted fares, which would require additional rollingstock to meet this demand. Any
additional rollingstock would be purchased in the More trains for the regions costing.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $250.4 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        ­   a decrease in revenue of $250.1 million due to a decrease in farebox revenue received from
            V/Line services

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $0.3 million due to advertising and communication related
            to the new regional fare prices

       $1,110.4 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        ­   a decrease in revenue of $1,110.1 million due to a decrease in farebox revenue received from
            V/Line services

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $0.3 million due to advertising and communication related
            to the new regional fare prices

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in:

       fare price

       estimated patronage demand policy impacts.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Post COVID-19, public transport revenue would reach around 80% of pre-COVID-19 levels by 2026–
   27 and thereafter increase in line with the Victorian budget’s population and consumer price index
   projections (trend).

2. This policy would not be affected by increases in capital and operating costs for the public
   transport system resulting from trend increases in patronage.

3. Peak patronage in 2018–19 represents the capacity of the network.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                Cheaper public transport fares for the regions


4. Increased patronage resulting from the change in fare price is capped at 2018–19 peak-period
   levels and therefore there is no increase in capital or operating costs of the public transport
   system.

5. Increased patronage resulting from the change in fare price affects full fare patronage first and
   then concession fare patronage.

6. Additional patrons take 2 trips per day on average.

7. Concession and full-fare patronage proportions would remain constant.

8. Regional public transport patronage responds according to price elasticity estimates.

9. There is no regional modal substitution effect as the price of all public transport modes are
   changing by the same amount.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

   estimated the impact of COVID-19 on public transport usage and the rate of patronage return in
    following years

   estimated the revenue foregone from existing patrons from a reduction in public transport fare
    prices

   estimated the additional patronage resulting from a reduction in fare prices

   estimated the revenue increase from new patrons using public transport due to reduced fare prices

   escalated the revenue foregone by relevant indexation.

We applied a part-year impact to the first year of this policy to reflect a proposed 31 March 2023
commencement date.

Data sources

   Australian Bureau of Statistics. 2022. Fewer Australians using public transport after COVID-19.
    [ONLINE] Available at: https://www.abs.gov.au/media-centre/media-releases/fewer-australians-
    using-public-transport-after-covid-19. [Accessed 21 November 2022]

   Elsevier. Evidence of a post-COVID change in travel behaviour – Self-reported expectations of
    commuting in Melbourne. 2021. [ONLINE] Available
    at: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0965856421002391?via%3Dihub.
    [Accessed 21 November 2022]

   Information provided by the Department of Transport

   Public Transport Victoria. 2019. 2018–19 Annual Report. [ONLINE] Available
    at: https://www.ptv.vic.gov.au/assets/PTV-default-site/footer/data-and-reporting/annual-
    report/PTVH4043-Annual_Report_2019-A4-v1_ONLINE.pdf. [Accessed 21 November 2022]

   Public Transport Victoria. 2019. 2021–22 Annual Report. [ONLINE] Available
    at: https://transport.vic.gov.au/about/our-annual-report-2021-2022. [Accessed 21 November 2022]

                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


       Public Transport Victoria. 2022. Growing Our Rail Network 2018–2025. [ONLINE] Available
        at: https://www.ptv.vic.gov.au/footer/legal-and-policies/growing-our-rail-network-2018-2025/.
        [Accessed 21 November 2022]

       Transport Victoria. 2022. Fares. [ONLINE] Available at: https://www.ptv.vic.gov.au/tickets/fares/.

    5
                                                                         OFFICIAL

                                                                                                   Cheaper public transport fares for the regions



Attachment A– Detailed budget impacts

                                                              Year-on-year impacts

Publicly-announced policy costing

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




 3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                              Delivering local road upgrades


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

   $61.7 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

    ­   an increase in assets of $50.2 million due to road related infrastructure

    ­   an increase in operating expenses of $11.4 million due to funding for grants and project
        planning.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $50.2 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24 to
completion in 2024–25.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the:

   final project design and construction profile

   road project escalation rates.

The financial implications of the capped components of this policy are certain as they relate to
specified capped funding. We made no judgement as to the efficacy of the funding specified to achieve
the desired outcomes.

This policy requires government departments to absorb administration costs. We have made no
judgement on the potential impact of this on Victorian Government services.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. The upgrades would take 2 years to complete.

2. The construction cost would increase in line with the relevant indexation.

3. For components of this policy that would install speed signs at and around pedestrian crossings,
   there would be an even number of signs in each direction.

4. The shared-user path between Montmorency and Eltham would follow the rail line between the
   stations.




                                                                                                         4
                                                   OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Method

When costing this policy, we:

       determined the upgrades required at each location in the costing request

       determined the type of pedestrian signals and crossings that would be required

       determined the route and length of the Eltham shared-user path

       estimated the cost of implementing each of the required upgrades based on previous similar
        projects

       estimated the output funding required for the upgrades.

We based the capped components of this policy costing on the capped amount of funding specified in
the election commitment.

As a result, we found the estimate of the Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

Election commitment            https://www.dtf.vic.gov.au/sites/default/files/document/Election%20
                               Costing%20Request%20042%20-
                               %20Australian%20Labor%20Party%20-
                               %20Delivering%20more%20local%20buses%20-

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Delivering more local buses



Our response

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                          2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $8.7 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in assets of $1.9 million due to purchasing additional buses

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $6.8 million due to the cost of running new services

       $11.6 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        ­   an increase in assets of $1.9 million due to purchasing additional buses

        ­   an increase in operating expenses of $9.8 million due to the cost of running the new services

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $1.9 million from the purchase of buses in 2023–24.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the uptake of the new service, bus route and number of
additional buses.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. New buses would be required to service the new bus route.

2. Frequency of the bus service would be similar to current bus services in the suburb

3. The average cost per service km would increase in line with the Victorian consumer price index.



Method

When costing this policy we:

       estimated the additional total km serviced by applying the distance of the bus route to the total
        number of extended services introduced

       estimated total expenditure by applying the additional total kilometres serviced to the average cost
        per service km, adjusted for escalation

       estimated total farebox revenue as a proportion of the total operating costs required to provide the
        extended service

       estimated the cost of adding new buses.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

                               ▪   Warrnambool Line – 5 return services a day on Saturday and
                                   Sunday
                               ▪   Ararat Line – 5 return services on Saturday and Sunday
                               ▪   Echuca Line – 3 return services on Saturday and Sunday
                               ▪   Gippsland Line – Extension of Sunday Sale service to Bairnsdale
                               The services on the Geelong line would commence on 1 July 2024,

Costing components

                                                                                   2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

This policy would increase public transport services in regional Victoria and in doing so it interacts with
the Australian Labor Party – Victorian Branch election policy costing:

▪       More trains for the regions

        -   An increased number of services, requiring additional rollingstock would be required to meet
            the newly created demand.

        -   Any additional rollingstock would be purchased in the More trains for the regions costing.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $87.8 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $113.6 million due to increasing the frequency of regional
            passenger services

        -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $25.8 million due to an increase in farebox revenue

▪       $527.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $717.6 million due to increasing the frequency of regional
            passenger services

        -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $190.5 million due to an increase in farebox
            revenue

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in:

▪       the Melbourne wage price index

▪       Behavioural impacts of passenger uptake for new services.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. There would be no additional infrastructure work required above what the Victorian Government is
   currently undertaking to accommodate the increase in services.

2. An increase in V/line services on lines where tracks are shared with the metropolitan network
   would not impact the service frequency of the metropolitan services.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                          Extra train services for the regions


Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined the number of services on each line with the additional services outlined in the policy
    summary

▪   estimated the cost of operating additional services

▪   estimated the cost of driver recruitment and training

▪   escalated these by the relevant indices

▪   estimated the farebox revenue from additional services.

                                             Free rego for our hard-working apprentices and tradies



Our response

Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                                2
                                                     OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Trade apprentices are currently eligible for a 50% discount of the car registration fee.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

       $7.1 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26

       $9.7 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33.

This is entirely due to a decrease in revenue due to reduced registration fees for trade apprentices.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Revenue foregone from registration fees would increase annually in line with the combined average
   projected average growth from 2022–23 to 2025–26 of the Melbourne consumer price index and
   Victorian population growth

2. This policy would have no impact on the annual demand on the registration renewal of vehicles.

3. Policy would apply over 4 years and would conclude in 2026–27 budget year.

Method

When costing this policy, we:

       estimated the reduction in revenue from the current 50% discount

       escalated the cost by relevant indexation.

Data sources

        Bureau of Statistics. [ONLINE] Available at: https://www.abs.gov.au/statistics/labour/employment-
        and-unemployment/labour-force-australia-detailed/latest-release. [Accessed 02 December 2022]

       Australian Labor Party – Victorian Division. 2022. 2022 Victorian Election Costings | Costing
        request | Department of Treasury and Finance Victoria. [ONLINE] Available at: Election Costing
        Request 025 - Australian Labor Party - Support for our hard-working apprentices and tradies - 9
        November 2022.pdf (dtf.vic.gov.au). [Accessed 24 November 2022]

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  More trains for the regions



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                          2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Context

Vlocity trains have been operating on Victoria’s regional rail network since 2005.

Most Vlocity trains are built for broad-gauge line operations, with a minority built for standard-gauge
lines. While each diesel-powered train currently operates as a 3-carriage set, the Victorian Government
plans to deploy 6-carriage and 9-carriage configurations in areas of high demand.

Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $443.3 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, reflecting an increase in assets due to rail rolling stock
        procurement.

▪       $697.9 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $601.1 million due to rail rolling stock procurement

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $96.8 million due to ongoing maintenance costs.

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $601.1 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2027–28.

This cost estimate is sensitive to the terms and conditions of rail rolling stock procurement contracts
and future operating costs.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. Each additional Vlocity train would be built according to an existing design and have 3 carriages

2. The total cost to acquire each of these trains would be consistent with previous purchases
   adjusted for price escalation

3. The capital expenditure profile would be consistent with previous Vlocity train procurement.

4. Other Vlocity train procurement initiatives would not affect the timeframe and cost of this policy.

5. Maintenance costs would be similar to those for other Victorian rolling stock.




    3
                                                OFFICIAL

                                                                                    More trains for the regions


Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined which rolling stock the policy would procure

▪   determined the profile of Vlocity train procurement initiatives in the budget

▪   estimated the capital costs and profile of the policy

▪   applied additional contingency funding in line with the Department of Treasury and Finance’s
    guidelines

▪   estimated the ongoing maintenance costs associated with the additional rolling stock.

As a result, we found the estimate of The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP to be reasonable.

        Publicly-announced policy costing

Road blitz to get families home sooner and safer

Political party        Australian Labor Party - Victorian Branch

Parliamentary leader   The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP

Policy commencement    1 July 2023

Policy conclusion      Ongoing

Policy summary         On 24 November 2022, The Hon. Daniel Andrews MP committed to deliver
                       a package of roads upgrades:
                       ▪   upgrade Point Cook Road, including upgrading the intersection at
                           Central Avenue
                       ▪   replace the roundabout at the intersection of Grant Street, Griffith
                           Street and Station Street in Bacchus Marsh with traffic lights
                       ▪   upgrade the intersection at Thompsons Road and Berwick-
                           Cranbourne Road in Clyde North with traffic lights
                       ▪   upgrade Ballan Road at the McGrath Road and Greens Road
                           intersection in Werribee
                       ▪   complete Stage 2 of the Barwon Heads Road Upgrade
                       ▪   upgrade the Hume Freeway interchange at Watson Street, Wallan to a
                           full diamond interchange and related upgrades to Watson Street
                       ▪   up to $10.0 million towards a business case for the upgrade of the
                           Western Highway between Melton and Caroline Springs.
                       This policy includes the following additional funding from the Australian
                       Government:
                       ▪   $125.0 million towards Stage 2 of the Barwon Heads Road Upgrade
                       ▪   $45.0 million towards the upgrade the Hume Freeway interchange at
                           Watson Street, Wallan to a full diamond interchange and related
                           upgrades to Watson Street.

                                       OFFICIAL

                                                  Road blitz to get families home sooner and safer



Our response
Budget impact




Costing components




Source: Parliamentary Budget Office.




                                                                                               2
                                                    OFFICIAL

Publicly-announced policy costing


Costing overview

We expect this policy to decrease the state’s budgeted net position by:

▪       $339.4 million across 2022–23 to 2025–26, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $431.4 million due to road infrastructure construction

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $10.0 million due to funding of the Western Highway
            business case

        -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $102.0 million due to Australian Government
            funding contributions

▪       $559.0 million across 2022–23 to 2032–33, this reflects:

        -   an increase in assets of $719.0 million due to road infrastructure construction

        -   an increase in operating expenses of $10.0 million due to funding of the Western Highway
            business case

        -   partially offset by an increase in revenue of $170.0 million due to Australian Government
            funding contributions

The total estimated investment (TEI) reflects the amount of expenditure needed to undertake the asset
works, but excludes the associated operating, maintenance expenses and finance lease costs. We
expect this policy to have a TEI of $719.0 million from the commencement of asset works in 2023–24
to completion in 2028–29.

This cost estimate is sensitive to changes in the final project design and construction profile.




Our approach

Assumptions

When costing this policy, we made the following assumptions:

1. There would be no additional earthworks beyond what is stipulated in Victorian Planning Authority
   benchmark costings.

2. There would be no relocation of utilities.

3. The Victorian Government would undertake all related works that are currently planned.

4. Project start dates would be staggered to ensure works are achievable.

5. Projects with capped costing amounts would incorporate administrative costs.

6. There would be no contaminated land or material that would require removal.

7. Project phasing would be consistent with similar projects.

8. Australian Government funding would be provided consistent with construction phasing.




    3
                                               OFFICIAL

                                                             Road blitz to get families home sooner and safer


Method

When costing this policy, we:

▪   determined the distance of road to be upgraded

▪   determined the number of new intersections, interchanges, overpasses, bridges and culverts to be
    built or upgraded

▪   estimated the costs of duplicating or upgrading the road

▪   estimated the cost of building or upgrading new intersections, interchanges, overpasses, bridges
    and culverts

▪   estimated the timing of Australian Government funding.
